- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GreenMind-Medium-14B-R1, a Vietnamese large language model (LLM) designed for structured and logical reasoning. The model is fine-tuned using a Vietnamese reasoning dataset and a Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) technique. The authors address limitations of existing GRPO approaches by incorporating reward functions to mitigate language mixing (biased towards dominant languages) and ensure factual correctness during reasoning. Experimental results on Vietnamese datasets and the multilingual SeaExam dataset show that GreenMind outperforms previous works and enhances linguistic consistency. The paper also emphasizes the creation of a high-quality Vietnamese reasoning dataset, a crucial element for their model's performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Focus on a Low-Resource Language:** The paper addresses a clear gap in the LLM landscape by focusing on Vietnamese, a language with relatively limited resources compared to English or Chinese. This is an important step towards making LLMs more accessible and relevant to diverse communities.
    *   **Addressing GRPO Limitations:** The authors identify and tackle specific limitations of GRPO, particularly language bias and content distortion, through the design of novel reward functions. This is a valuable contribution that improves the applicability and reliability of GRPO in multilingual settings.
    *   **Dataset Curation:** The creation and release of a high-quality Vietnamese reasoning dataset is a significant contribution, as it provides a valuable resource for training and evaluating LLMs in Vietnamese.
    *   **Improved Performance:** The experimental results demonstrate that GreenMind outperforms prior works on Vietnamese datasets and exhibits better linguistic consistency. Its competitive performance on the multilingual SeaExam dataset further validates the effectiveness of the proposed reasoning method.
    *   **Technically Sound:** The paper details the architecture, training process, and reward functions, allowing other researchers to reproduce and build upon their work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the paper combines several techniques (GRPO, custom reward functions, targeted dataset), the individual components are not entirely novel. The main novelty lies in the specific application and adaptation of these techniques to the Vietnamese language and the identified limitations.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** The paper could benefit from more detailed ablation studies to isolate the impact of each reward function and the GRPO technique on overall performance. This would help to better understand the contributions of each component.
    *   **Comparison Models:** The baseline comparison models, while including known existing models are slightly ambiguous as some are not clearly defined and do not include all available models. For a more rigorous comparison, it would be beneficial to include other well-established open-source models specific to Vietnamese language processing, not necessarily a direct comparison to the SeaExam.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the development of more inclusive and linguistically diverse LLMs.
    *   The proposed techniques for mitigating language bias and ensuring factual correctness have broader implications for training LLMs in other low-resource languages.
    *   The released dataset serves as a valuable resource for the Vietnamese NLP community, potentially fostering further research and development.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by focusing on a low-resource language, addressing limitations of existing techniques, and providing a valuable dataset. However, the novelty is somewhat incremental, and the paper could benefit from more in-depth analysis and comparison against benchmarks.

Score: 7/10

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LRASGen: LLM-based RESTful API Specification Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16833v1)
- **Authors**: Sida Deng, Rubing Huang, Man Zhang, Chenhui Cui, Dave Towey, Rongcun Wang
- **Abstract**: REpresentation State Transfer (REST) is an architectural style for designing web applications that enable scalable, stateless communication between clients and servers via common HTTP techniques. Web APIs that employ the REST style are known as RESTful (or REST) APIs. When using or testing a RESTful API, developers may need to employ its specification, which is often defined by open-source standards such as the OpenAPI Specification (OAS). However, it can be very time-consuming and error-prone to write and update these specifications, which may negatively impact the use of RESTful APIs, especially when the software requirements change. Many tools and methods have been proposed to solve this problem, such as Respector and Swagger Core. OAS generation can be regarded as a common text-generation task that creates a formal description of API endpoints derived from the source code. A potential solution for this may involve using Large Language Models (LLMs), which have strong capabilities in both code understanding and text generation. Motivated by this, we propose a novel approach for generating the OASs of RESTful APIs using LLMs: LLM-based RESTful API-Specification Generation (LRASGen). To the best of our knowledge, this is the first use of LLMs and API source code to generate OASs for RESTful APIs. Compared with existing tools and methods, LRASGen can generate the OASs, even when the implementation is incomplete (with partial code, and/or missing annotations/comments, etc.). To evaluate the LRASGen performance, we conducted a series of empirical studies on 20 real-world RESTful APIs. The results show that two LLMs (GPT-4o mini and DeepSeek V3) can both support LARSGen to generate accurate specifications, and LRASGen-generated specifications cover an average of 48.85% more missed entities than the developer-provided specifications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LRASGen, a novel approach for automatically generating OpenAPI specifications (OAS) from RESTful API source code using Large Language Models (LLMs).  The method extracts and cleans endpoint code files, then uses the LLM (GPT-40 mini and DeepSeek V3) to identify endpoint methods, parameters, constraints, and responses. The authors compare LRASGen's performance against developer-provided specifications and state-of-the-art OAS generation techniques (Respector, Swagger Core, Springdoc) across 20 real-world APIs (Java, Python, C#). The results demonstrate that LRASGen achieves high accuracy and completeness, often exceeding existing techniques and even uncovering elements missing from developer-provided specifications. The paper also details the framework of LRASGen, experiments, and limitations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** Using LLMs for automatic OpenAPI specification generation is the core novelty. While LLMs have been used for code understanding and generation tasks previously, this paper appears to be one of the first to directly address the API specification generation problem. The framework itself (prompt engineering, code cleanup) has elements of existing code analysis techniques, but the integrated use of LLMs for this specific purpose is what distinguishes it.
*   **Significance:** Automating OAS generation is significant due to the high cost of manually maintaining them. If LRASGen and similar tools can reliably produce complete and accurate OASs, it could reduce developer workload, improve API usability, and prevent integration issues. The paper's ability to address cases of incomplete code or missing annotations further enhances its significance, as these are common pain points in API development.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a robust experimental evaluation on a diverse set of real-world APIs, including different languages and frameworks.
    *   **Strong Results:** The results convincingly demonstrate LRASGen's high accuracy and completeness compared to existing methods. The ability to identify entities missed by developers underscores the practical value.
    *   **Clear Framework and Methodology:**  The paper provides a detailed explanation of the LRASGen framework, including the prompt engineering process, making it reproducible.
    *   **Addresses a Practical Problem:** The issue of OAS maintenance and accuracy is a well-known problem in the API development lifecycle.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on LLMs:** The performance of LRASGen is directly tied to the capabilities of the underlying LLM.  Future improvements or regressions in LLM performance will directly affect LRASGen's effectiveness. The reliance on commercial LLMs introduces a cost factor and potential vulnerability to API changes or restrictions from the providers.
    *   **Limited Generalization Details**: While the method is applicable across languages, the framework-specific criteria for identifying endpoint entry files seem to rely on developer input.
    *   **Limited Scope of Comparison:** Comparing against tools only applicable to Java-based APIs introduces bias into RQ3. A more comprehensive comparison that extends to tools applicable to Python or C# is needed to fully evaluate generalization capability.
    *   **Lack of Analysis for failure cases:** The results are presented with good precision and recall metrics. However, a deeper analysis of the cases where the LLM fails is lacking.
    *  **Threats to Validity:** While the authors discuss threats to validity, they lack a comprehensive discussion of bias. It is difficult to assess the generality of their approach when the APIs used are all popular, open source and well maintained.

**Justification of Score:**

LRASGen represents a valuable contribution to automated API specification generation by effectively leveraging LLMs. The comprehensive experimental validation and clear methodological description are strengths of the paper. However, the reliance on commercial LLMs, the dependency on high quality source code and lack of analysis for failure cases prevents it from being a truly exceptional result.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Physically Consistent Humanoid Loco-Manipulation using Latent Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16843v1)
- **Authors**: Ilyass Taouil, Haizhou Zhao, Angela Dai, Majid Khadiv
- **Abstract**: This paper uses the capabilities of latent diffusion models (LDMs) to generate realistic RGB human-object interaction scenes to guide humanoid loco-manipulation planning. To do so, we extract from the generated images both the contact locations and robot configurations that are then used inside a whole-body trajectory optimization (TO) formulation to generate physically consistent trajectories for humanoids. We validate our full pipeline in simulation for different long-horizon loco-manipulation scenarios and perform an extensive analysis of the proposed contact and robot configuration extraction pipeline. Our results show that using the information extracted from LDMs, we can generate physically consistent trajectories that require long-horizon reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel pipeline for humanoid loco-manipulation planning that leverages latent diffusion models (LDMs) to generate realistic human-object interaction scenes. These scenes are then used to extract contact locations and robot configurations, which are fed into a whole-body trajectory optimization (TO) framework. The pipeline aims to generate physically consistent trajectories for long-horizon tasks. The paper validates its approach through simulations involving two scenarios: retrieving a laundry basket from a shelf and moving a box using a trolley.  The experiments compare the proposed method against baselines and ablations, demonstrating the benefits of using LDM-derived information and refining contacts geometrically.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its integration of LDMs with traditional trajectory optimization for humanoid loco-manipulation.  While LDMs have been used for scene generation and robot task planning before, the specific application to *humanoid* loco-manipulation, combined with contact extraction and whole-body TO, is a relatively unexplored area.  The approach is also novel in that it *doesn't* rely on task-specific heuristics or pre-existing 3D interaction datasets. The refinement process for contact location extraction using both semantic information *and* point cloud geometry is another novel aspect. However, the individual components (LDMs, contact extraction, TO) are not entirely new; the *combination* and adaptation for humanoid robots represent the core innovation.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in robotics: planning complex, long-horizon tasks for humanoids, which are high-dimensional and dynamically unstable. The use of LDMs to generate plausible human-like behaviors offers a potentially valuable approach to guide robot planning and overcome the limitations of purely local optimization methods.  Demonstrating the pipeline's effectiveness in simulation is a crucial first step. If this method can translate effectively to real-world scenarios, it could significantly accelerate progress in deploying humanoid robots for complex manipulation tasks.  However, the reliance on simulation and the specific scenarios tested limit the scope of demonstrating broad applicability. The paper also lacks real-world validation, which limits its immediate impact.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges in humanoid loco-manipulation.
    * **Well-Defined Pipeline:**  The proposed pipeline is well-structured and modular, making it easier to understand and potentially extend.
    * **Empirical Validation:**  The simulation results demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach compared to baselines and ablations. The authors provide quantitative metrics (collision penetration) to support their claims.
    * **Addresses Long-Horizon Reasoning:** The incorporation of LDMs helps the system make decisions across a longer planning horizon.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Simulation-Only Validation:** The lack of real-world experiments is a major limitation.  The transition from simulation to reality can be challenging due to factors such as sensor noise, model inaccuracies, and dynamic uncertainties.
    * **Limited Scenario Diversity:** The two scenarios, while challenging, are still relatively constrained.  The generalizability of the approach to a wider range of tasks and environments needs to be investigated.
    * **Dependency on LDM Quality:** The performance of the pipeline is directly dependent on the quality of the images generated by the LDM. Imperfections in the generated images could lead to inaccurate contact extraction and degraded performance. The method to append text to the initial prompts to obtain a full-body person description is not fully automated or robust.
    * **IK Regularization Simplification:** The paper mentions using the joint angles as a regularization term. This is a simplification as human and robot kinematics can significantly differ.
    * **Computational Cost:** The paper doesn't thoroughly address the computational cost of the LDM image generation and processing pipeline, which could be a bottleneck for real-time applications.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper's approach could influence future research in humanoid robot planning by encouraging the use of generative models to provide high-level guidance and overcome the limitations of purely reactive or local planning methods.  It also highlights the importance of extracting and refining contact information for robust loco-manipulation.

* **Score:** 7

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and interesting integration of LDMs into a humanoid loco-manipulation planning pipeline. The simulation results are promising, and the paper addresses a significant problem in robotics. However, the lack of real-world validation, the limitations of the tested scenarios, the dependency on LDM quality, and the computational cost concerns prevent a higher score. While the approach is promising, significant work is needed to demonstrate its practical applicability and robustness. The paper has a high likelihood of being cited and influencing future research directions, but its immediate impact is limited by the mentioned weaknesses.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Hyperspectral Vision Transformers for Greenhouse Gas Estimations from Space
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16851v1)
- **Authors**: Ruben Gonzalez Avilés, Linus Scheibenreif, Nassim Ait Ali Braham, Benedikt Blumenstiel, Thomas Brunschwiler, Ranjini Guruprasad, Damian Borth, Conrad Albrecht, Paolo Fraccaro, Devyani Lambhate, Johannes Jakubik
- **Abstract**: Hyperspectral imaging provides detailed spectral information and holds significant potential for monitoring of greenhouse gases (GHGs). However, its application is constrained by limited spatial coverage and infrequent revisit times. In contrast, multispectral imaging offers broader spatial and temporal coverage but often lacks the spectral detail that can enhance GHG detection. To address these challenges, this study proposes a spectral transformer model that synthesizes hyperspectral data from multispectral inputs. The model is pre-trained via a band-wise masked autoencoder and subsequently fine-tuned on spatio-temporally aligned multispectral-hyperspectral image pairs. The resulting synthetic hyperspectral data retain the spatial and temporal benefits of multispectral imagery and improve GHG prediction accuracy relative to using multispectral data alone. This approach effectively bridges the trade-off between spectral resolution and coverage, highlighting its potential to advance atmospheric monitoring by combining the strengths of hyperspectral and multispectral systems with self-supervised deep learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a method to synthesize hyperspectral data from more readily available multispectral data using a spectral transformer model. The model is pre-trained using a masked autoencoder approach on hyperspectral data and then fine-tuned on spatially and temporally aligned multispectral and hyperspectral image pairs. The authors hypothesize that this approach can bridge the gap between the high spectral resolution of hyperspectral imagery and the broad spatial and temporal coverage of multispectral imagery, improving greenhouse gas (GHG) estimation. They demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach by evaluating the reconstructed hyperspectral data in a downstream GHG estimation task, showing improved performance compared to using only multispectral data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of synthesizing hyperspectral data from multispectral data isn't entirely new. Spectral super-resolution techniques have been explored before. However, the use of a spectral transformer model, pre-trained with a masked autoencoder framework, specifically for hyperspectral reconstruction in the context of GHG estimation, represents a novel combination. The use of a masked autoencoder for pretraining of hyperspectral data is a fairly standard practice, and the specific architecture doesn't appear to introduce significant innovation.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work lies in its ability to leverage widely available multispectral data (e.g., Sentinel-2, Landsat) to improve atmospheric monitoring capabilities. Hyperspectral data offers finer spectral detail, which is crucial for accurate GHG detection, but its limited coverage poses a challenge. If this method can reliably synthesize hyperspectral data, it would allow for more frequent and spatially comprehensive GHG monitoring, which is vital for understanding and addressing climate change. The success in reconstructing finer spectral details from readily available multispectral data is pivotal for downstream tasks such as precise mineral exploration and environmental monitoring. The paper adequately highlights the trade-off between the spectral resolution and the spatial/temporal coverage, thus emphasizing the importance of addressing such limitations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly outlines the problem and the proposed solution.
    *   The use of both real and synthetic multispectral data allows for a more controlled evaluation.
    *   The three-stage training framework (pre-training, fine-tuning, downstream task) is well-structured.
    *   The paper comprehensively discusses the experimental setup, data processing steps, and evaluation metrics.
    *   The authors analyze the results in detail, providing plausible explanations for the observed trends.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The improvement in GHG estimation is marginal, particularly for methane. While the results indicate some improvement over multispectral data, the margins are not dramatically significant, and in one case, for CO2, the results are considered inconclusive. This raises questions about the robustness of the approach and its practical utility. The inconsistent improvement across different GHGs and datasets implies that the model's generalizability might be limited and potentially dependent on the specific GHG and dataset characteristics.
    *   The paper acknowledges limitations regarding the CO2 dataset size. However, the limitations in performance even with larger datasets (like methane) aren't fully addressed.
    *   The results for real Sentinel-2 data are less impressive than for synthetic data, indicating potential challenges in generalizing the model to real-world scenarios due to confounding factors like atmospheric effects and sensor differences. This also points to a need for a more robust reconstruction approach, considering that satellite data is inherently noisy.
    *   The novelty of the spectral transformer architecture itself is not particularly emphasized. It's more of an application of existing transformer techniques to a specific problem, so its architectural contribution to the field of vision transformers is minimal.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could potentially inspire further research in spectral super-resolution and its applications in remote sensing. However, its impact will depend on whether the method can be improved to achieve more substantial and consistent gains in downstream tasks, especially with real-world data. Future research directions include exploring more sophisticated transformer architectures, incorporating atmospheric correction techniques, and evaluating the approach on a wider range of datasets and applications. In particular, future studies can focus on improving the model's generalizability to satellite data and addressing factors such as noise in satellite data.
    *   The current results are not strong enough to immediately influence industry practices.

*   **Conclusion:** This work demonstrates a promising approach to synthesize hyperspectral data from multispectral imagery, but the marginal improvements in GHG estimation and the inconsistencies across datasets suggest that the method needs further refinement. The use of a masked autoencoder for pre-training, while useful, isn't particularly novel.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**
The paper presents a well-executed study on a relevant problem, but the results lack the degree of improvement necessary to justify a higher score. The use of a spectral transformer is a logical extension of existing techniques, but the novelty isn't groundbreaking. The improvements in GHG estimation, while present, are not substantial enough to indicate a significant breakthrough. The limitations in generalizing to real-world Sentinel-2 data also impact the overall score. While the work has potential for future development, the current results are incremental.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Monte Carlo Planning with Large Language Model for Text-Based Game Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16855v1)
- **Authors**: Zijing Shi, Meng Fang, Ling Chen
- **Abstract**: Text-based games provide valuable environments for language-based autonomous agents. However, planning-then-learning paradigms, such as those combining Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) and reinforcement learning (RL), are notably time-consuming due to extensive iterations. Additionally, these algorithms perform uncertainty-driven exploration but lack language understanding and reasoning abilities. In this paper, we introduce the Monte Carlo planning with Dynamic Memory-guided Large language model (MC-DML) algorithm. MC-DML leverages the language understanding and reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) alongside the exploratory advantages of tree search algorithms. Specifically, we enhance LLMs with in-trial and cross-trial memory mechanisms, enabling them to learn from past experiences and dynamically adjust action evaluations during planning. We conduct experiments on a series of text-based games from the Jericho benchmark. Our results demonstrate that the MC-DML algorithm significantly enhances performance across various games at the initial planning phase, outperforming strong contemporary methods that require multiple iterations. This demonstrates the effectiveness of our algorithm, paving the way for more efficient language-grounded planning in complex environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Monte Carlo Planning with Dynamic Memory-guided Large Language Model (MC-DML) for text-based game agents. This algorithm enhances the traditional Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) by integrating the language understanding and reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). MC-DML uses in-trial and cross-trial memory mechanisms to enable the LLM to learn from past experiences and dynamically adjust action evaluations during the planning process. The authors evaluate MC-DML on a suite of text-based games from the Jericho benchmark and demonstrate that it outperforms existing methods in terms of performance at the initial planning phase.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the integration of LLMs within an MCTS framework *and* the specific implementation of dynamic memory mechanisms (in-trial and cross-trial) to allow the LLM policy to adapt during planning. While LLM-augmented MCTS is not entirely new (as the authors themselves acknowledge with LLM-MCTS), the dynamic memory component is a significant contribution. The ability to reflect on failure trajectories and adjust action probabilities based on past experiences is a crucial enhancement for complex, uncertain environments like text-based games.

*   **Significance:** Text-based games represent a challenging domain for AI agents due to the vast action spaces, sparse rewards, and the need for natural language understanding. The success of MC-DML in this domain signifies an important step toward more efficient and adaptable language-grounded planning. The ability to learn quickly from limited experience is particularly valuable, addressing a key limitation of traditional reinforcement learning methods. The reported results highlight that the developed algorithm addresses the initial-planning performance deficiency, outperforming existing, more iterative techniques.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of planning in text-based games and the limitations of existing methods.
    *   **Well-Defined Algorithm:** The MC-DML algorithm is described in detail, with a clear explanation of the memory mechanisms and their role in action selection. The algorithm description is presented in a way that allows for reproduction.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct experiments on a variety of games and compare MC-DML to a range of baselines, including RL-based, LLM-based, and MCTS-based approaches.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of the memory mechanisms and dynamic pruning. The performed ablations are well-designed.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:** The inclusion of a qualitative analysis, showcasing how MC-DML addresses bottleneck states, adds depth to the evaluation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on LLMs:** The algorithm is inherently tied to the capabilities of the underlying LLM. Improvements in LLMs would directly translate to performance gains for MC-DML, but any limitations of the LLM (e.g., bias, hallucination) could also affect the agent's behavior.
    *   **Limited Long-Term Memory Exploration:** The paper mentions that the in-trial memory is limited to shorter time windows, placing demands on the LLM's ability to retrieve past information.
    *   **Scalability Concerns:** While effective, the method has scalability concerns related to computational cost and time, as the authors acknowledged the need for improvements in in-trial memory storage and retrieval mechanisms.
    *   **Limited parameter tuning details** Although configuration followed the work of Jang et al. (2020), details relating to parameter optimization are limited and thus reproducibility could be challenging.
    *   **No statistical significance tests:** The evaluation is limited to reporting the mean score, however, statistical significance tests are not mentioned.
    *   **Limited qualitative analysis:** The qualitative analysis includes only a single-trajectory example, which does not provide a convincing argument.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of language-grounded AI by demonstrating how LLMs can be effectively integrated into planning frameworks. It could inspire further research on memory mechanisms for LLM-based agents and the development of more efficient and adaptable planning algorithms for complex environments.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of the dynamic memory mechanism and its impact on performance, the comprehensive evaluation, and the potential influence on future research, I would assign this paper a score of **7.5**. While the reliance on LLMs and potential scalability concerns prevent a higher score, the paper represents a solid contribution to the field. The well-designed ablation studies bolster the claims. The clear writing and comprehensive evaluation of the algorithm are commendable.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Emo Pillars: Knowledge Distillation to Support Fine-Grained Context-Aware and Context-Less Emotion Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16856v1)
- **Authors**: Alexander Shvets
- **Abstract**: Most datasets for sentiment analysis lack context in which an opinion was expressed, often crucial for emotion understanding, and are mainly limited by a few emotion categories. Foundation large language models (LLMs) like GPT-4 suffer from over-predicting emotions and are too resource-intensive. We design an LLM-based data synthesis pipeline and leverage a large model, Mistral-7b, for the generation of training examples for more accessible, lightweight BERT-type encoder models. We focus on enlarging the semantic diversity of examples and propose grounding the generation into a corpus of narratives to produce non-repetitive story-character-centered utterances with unique contexts over 28 emotion classes. By running 700K inferences in 450 GPU hours, we contribute with the dataset of 100K contextual and also 300K context-less examples to cover both scenarios. We use it for fine-tuning pre-trained encoders, which results in several Emo Pillars models. We show that Emo Pillars models are highly adaptive to new domains when tuned to specific tasks such as GoEmotions, ISEAR, IEMOCAP, and EmoContext, reaching the SOTA performance on the first three. We also validate our dataset, conducting statistical analysis and human evaluation, and confirm the success of our measures in utterance diversification (although less for the neutral class) and context personalization, while pointing out the need for improved handling of out-of-taxonomy labels within the pipeline.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, focusing on the requested criteria:

**Summary:**

The paper "Emo Pillars: Knowledge Distillation to Support Fine-Grained Context-Aware and Context-Less Emotion Classification" addresses the limitations of existing sentiment analysis datasets, which often lack contextual information and fine-grained emotion categories.  The authors propose a novel LLM-based data synthesis pipeline to generate a large dataset (100K contextual and 300K context-less examples) with diverse semantic content across 28 emotion classes, using Mistral-7b as the LLM engine. They achieve this by grounding generation in narrative corpora, evoking diverse perspectives, and generating multiple utterances simultaneously. This data is then used to fine-tune smaller BERT-type models, resulting in "Emo Pillars" models that achieve SOTA performance on several emotion classification tasks (GoEmotions, ISEAR, IEMOCAP), especially in context-aware scenarios. The authors conduct thorough analyses including statistical analysis, human evaluation, and demonstrate the success of diversification and personalization strategies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty and Significance:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novel Data Synthesis Pipeline:** The combination of grounding in narrative contexts, varying character perspectives, and generating multiple outputs, combined with a unique method for data synthesis is a significant contribution, particularly given the challenges of creating diverse and contextually rich emotional datasets.
    *   **Addressing LLM Limitations:** The paper tackles a practical problem: the high resource demands and over-prediction tendencies of large LLMs for emotion classification.  Distilling knowledge into smaller, more efficient models has clear value.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** Achieving SOTA performance on several standard emotion classification datasets validates the effectiveness of the generated data and the fine-tuning approach.
    *   **Publicly Available Resources:** Openly releasing the code, dataset, and models is crucial for reproducibility and further research in the community.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The paper demonstrates a rigorous approach, encompassing statistical validation, human evaluation (including assessments of neutrality and context relevance), and topic diversity analysis. The error analyses are particularly insightful.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Single LLM Reliance:** Although the approach of using LLMs to generate data is novel, a notable limitation is its reliance on a single LLM (Mistral-7b). While tests with GPT-3.5 were performed at the prompting stage, it would be better to see if other LLMs could provide diverse data (e.g. if the LLM has a certain bias, this could effect the generated data). In the 'Limitations' section the authors state that the reliance on only a single LLM allows for easier identification of potential issues with the outputs, this is a valid point.
    *   **Subjectivity and Label Noise:** Emotion is inherently subjective, and even with the authors' careful methods, there is likely to be label noise in the generated dataset. The human evaluation, while insightful, highlights discrepancies in perceived neutrality and indicates issues with some emotion assignments stemming from the mapping process.
    *   **Limited Real-World Validation:** While the results on established datasets are impressive, the use case of applying the models to YouTube comments is presented somewhat superficially. More detailed analysis of how the models perform in a real-world setting (e.g., deployment, A/B testing, user feedback) would strengthen the practical relevance claim.
    *   **Imbalance Mitigation Needed:** The authors note that only a few emotions have small representation, so mitigation of class imbalance is required (similar to goEmotions). This is an area that needs improvement.
    *   **Generalizability Considerations** The model relies on English data (often English-centered or biased towards dominant cultural factors within a language). This can affect cultural background within a language or across other languages, further consideration is needed.

**Overall Significance:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of emotion classification by addressing the data scarcity problem and providing a pathway to build more efficient and context-aware models. The detailed analysis and publicly available resources are assets to the community.

**Justification of Score:**

The novelty and significance of the Emo Pillars data pipeline and the achieved performance on multiple datasets (especially context-aware ones) merit a high score. While limitations related to label noise, LLM dependency, real-world validation, and the reliance on English are important to acknowledge, the authors provide a novel synthesis pipeline that delivers considerable added value for the AI community.

**Score: 8/10**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Planning with Diffusion Models for Target-Oriented Dialogue Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16858v1)
- **Authors**: Hanwen Du, Bo Peng, Xia Ning
- **Abstract**: Target-Oriented Dialogue (TOD) remains a significant challenge in the LLM era, where strategic dialogue planning is crucial for directing conversations toward specific targets. However, existing dialogue planning methods generate dialogue plans in a step-by-step sequential manner, and may suffer from compounding errors and myopic actions. To address these limitations, we introduce a novel dialogue planning framework, DiffTOD, which leverages diffusion models to enable non-sequential dialogue planning. DiffTOD formulates dialogue planning as a trajectory generation problem with conditional guidance, and leverages a diffusion language model to estimate the likelihood of the dialogue trajectory. To optimize the dialogue action strategies, DiffTOD introduces three tailored guidance mechanisms for different target types, offering flexible guidance towards diverse TOD targets at test time. Extensive experiments across three diverse TOD settings show that DiffTOD can effectively perform non-myopic lookahead exploration and optimize action strategies over a long horizon through non-sequential dialogue planning, and demonstrates strong flexibility across complex and diverse dialogue scenarios. Our code and data are accessible through https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DiffTOD.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Planning with Diffusion Models for Target-Oriented Dialogue Systems":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DiffTOD, a novel framework for dialogue planning in target-oriented dialogue (TOD) systems.  Unlike traditional methods that generate dialogue plans sequentially (step-by-step), DiffTOD leverages diffusion models to enable *non-sequential* dialogue planning. It formulates dialogue planning as a trajectory generation problem conditioned on the dialogue history and the desired target. DiffTOD uses a masked diffusion language model to estimate the likelihood of a dialogue trajectory and introduces three different guidance mechanisms tailored to different types of TOD targets: word-level, semantic-level, and search-based guidance.  Experiments across three diverse TOD settings (negotiation, recommendation, and chitchat) demonstrate that DiffTOD outperforms baselines, showing improved target achievement and flexibility.  The key idea is that by planning the entire dialogue trajectory at once, DiffTOD can perform non-myopic lookahead exploration and optimize action strategies over a longer horizon.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty of the paper lies in the application of diffusion models to non-sequential dialogue planning. While diffusion models have been used for text generation, their use in *planning* the entire dialogue trajectory at once is a significant departure from traditional sequential approaches. The three guidance mechanisms (word, semantic, and search) add another layer of novelty, tailored to different TOD target types. This framework appears to be the first to exploit diffusion models for end-to-end dialogue planning with explicit target guidance. The conceptual link between inpainting with diffusion models and dialogue planning trajectory reconstruction is insightful.
*   **Significance:** TOD systems that can proactively guide conversations are critical for real-world applications. This paper's approach directly addresses the limitations of LLMs being passive followers of instructions. The claimed benefits of DiffTOD such as improved target achievement, global consistency, and flexibility are important for the robustness and effectiveness of dialogue systems. The extensive experimental results, covering multiple TOD settings, provide strong evidence for the significance of the proposed method. The framework has the potential to impact the design of future LLM-based dialogue agents, pushing them towards more strategic and goal-oriented behavior.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of sequential planning in TOD and motivates the need for non-sequential approaches.
    *   **Well-defined methodology:** The DiffTOD framework is well-defined, with a clear explanation of the diffusion model integration and the different guidance mechanisms.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The experiments are comprehensive, covering three diverse TOD settings and comparing against strong baselines.
    *   **Strong empirical results:** The results consistently show that DiffTOD outperforms baselines in terms of target achievement and flexibility.
    *   **Insightful discussion:** The paper provides a valuable discussion of the properties of DiffTOD (non-sequential planning, flexible guidance, tackling sparse rewards, modeling long-range dependencies) and how they contribute to the overall performance.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational cost:** As with most diffusion models, the inference cost can be relatively high compared to autoregressive methods. While the paper mentions potential solutions like acceleration techniques, a more detailed analysis of the practical cost implications would be beneficial. The "Inference Cost" section in the appendix provides some insight but should be expanded and directly tied to real-world deployment concerns.
    *   **Dependence on LLM Quality:** The semantic-level guidance mechanism, for example, relies on the ability of a powerful LLM (GPT-4o in this case) to generate good paraphrased versions of the target state. The performance of this component is therefore directly tied to the quality of the underlying LLM, and performance would likely degrade with smaller LLMs or open source LLMs.
    *   **Scalability to very complex dialogues:** While the paper covers different TOD settings, it is unclear how DiffTOD would scale to dialogues with extremely complex goals or longer interaction histories. There is a limit to how much information can be effectively encoded in the dialogue trajectory, even with a diffusion language model.
    *   **Lack of human evaluation detail:** In "H Human Evaluation Results on the CraigslistBargain Dataset" section, it mentions, "We recruit 5 independent volunteer annotators" - not much other details were provided.
*   **Potential Influence:** The work provides a compelling alternative to sequential planning in TOD.  The core idea of using diffusion models for trajectory generation and incorporating different guidance strategies has the potential to influence future research in dialogue planning and other related areas, such as reinforcement learning for dialogue policy optimization. The framework's modular design makes it relatively easy to extend or adapt to different TOD settings or tasks.

**Score:** 8/10

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of target-oriented dialogue systems. The application of diffusion models for non-sequential dialogue planning is a notable innovation, and the experimental results provide strong evidence for its effectiveness. While the computational cost remains a concern, the paper's strengths in terms of problem definition, methodology, and empirical validation outweigh its weaknesses. The potential influence on future research in dialogue planning is substantial. However, this work could be strengthened with a larger number of volunteers for human study, as well as more detail in the "Inference Cost" section.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Exploring How LLMs Capture and Represent Domain-Specific Knowledge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16871v2)
- **Authors**: Mirian Hipolito Garcia, Camille Couturier, Daniel Madrigal Diaz, Ankur Mallick, Anastasios Kyrillidis, Robert Sim, Victor Ruhle, Saravan Rajmohan
- **Abstract**: We study whether Large Language Models (LLMs) inherently capture domain-specific nuances in natural language. Our experiments probe the domain sensitivity of LLMs by examining their ability to distinguish queries from different domains using hidden states generated during the prefill phase. We reveal latent domain-related trajectories that indicate the model's internal recognition of query domains. We also study the robustness of these domain representations to variations in prompt styles and sources. Our approach leverages these representations for model selection, mapping the LLM that best matches the domain trace of the input query (i.e., the model with the highest performance on similar traces). Our findings show that LLMs can differentiate queries for related domains, and that the fine-tuned model is not always the most accurate. Unlike previous work, our interpretations apply to both closed and open-ended generative tasks
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores whether Large Language Models (LLMs) inherently capture and represent domain-specific knowledge during the prefill phase (input token processing). The authors probe this by analyzing the hidden states of LLMs, examining their ability to distinguish queries from different domains based on these hidden state activations. They reveal latent domain-related trajectories in these hidden states, indicating the model's internal recognition of query domains. The study also investigates the robustness of these domain representations to variations in prompt styles and sources. Finally, the authors propose a method for model selection based on these representations, mapping the LLM that best matches the domain trace of the input query. The results suggest that LLMs can differentiate queries from related domains and that a fine-tuned model isn't always the most accurate choice.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents an interesting approach to understanding how LLMs represent domain-specific knowledge. While the exploration of hidden states is not entirely novel in the LLM interpretability literature, the focus on domain sensitivity and the proposed application for model selection contribute meaningfully. The key strengths and weaknesses are as follows:

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Research Question and Methodology:** The paper clearly articulates the research question – how LLMs adapt reasoning across domains – and provides a well-defined methodology for investigating this, including the operational definitions of key terms (hidden states, mean/variance activations).
*   **Analysis Across Multiple LLMs:** The analysis spans several LLM architectures (Gemma, Phi, Mistral, Llama), lending credibility to the generalizability of the findings. The comparison with the DeBERTa encoder is also insightful, highlighting differences in representation between encoder and autoregressive models.
*   **Robustness Analysis:** The exploration of robustness across prompt styles and after fine-tuning is crucial. This addresses the concern that the observed patterns might be tied to specific prompt formats rather than genuine domain understanding.
*   **Practical Application (Model Selection):**  The paper presents a practical application of the research findings in the form of a model selection method. The improvement in accuracy demonstrated by the "LLM Hidden States Classifier" is encouraging.
*   **Comparison to Baselines:** The comparison against semantic routing and token-based classification methods provides a quantitative assessment of the proposed method's value.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Domains:** The study primarily focuses on four relatively broad domains (maths, biomedical, law, humanities). A more granular analysis within these domains (e.g., different areas of mathematics) would strengthen the conclusions.
*   **Generalizability to Larger LLMs:** The authors acknowledge that the work focuses on relatively smaller LLMs. It remains unclear whether the same patterns would emerge in much larger models with different training data and architectures. This is a critical limitation, as the trend in LLM research is towards ever-larger models.
*   **Overlapping Domains:**  The initial clustering of Math and Biomedical requires further investigation and is only briefly addressed. The paper needs a more robust discussion of the relationship and distinguishing features of domains.
*   **Interpretability of Latent Trajectories:** While the paper identifies domain-related trajectories, the precise meaning and content of these trajectories remain somewhat opaque. A deeper dive into *what* information the hidden states are encoding, beyond just "domain sensitivity," would be valuable.
*   **Black Box Approach:** The MLP classifier used to discriminate domain traces is a relatively simple model. A more sophisticated approach (e.g., using a Transformer-based classifier) might yield even better performance and provide further insights into the structure of the hidden states.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of LLM interpretability by demonstrating the domain sensitivity of hidden states and using this information for model selection.  The finding that domain-specific traces persist even after fine-tuning is particularly interesting.  However, the reliance on relatively small models and broad domains somewhat limits the impact.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a well-executed study with a clear research question, sound methodology, and interesting results. The model selection approach has practical potential. However, the novelty is incremental rather than revolutionary, and the limitations regarding model size and domain granularity hold it back from being a truly groundbreaking work. The paper demonstrates solid progress in understanding LLM internal representations, but needs to be expanded on in future work to increase its overall significance within the field. The findings, while promising, are limited by the dataset (MMLU is already well-known) and scope and do not challenge the existing assumptions about LLMs significantly.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Context-Enhanced Vulnerability Detection Based on Large Language Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16877v1)
- **Authors**: Yixin Yang, Bowen Xu, Xiang Gao, Hailong Sun
- **Abstract**: Vulnerability detection is a critical aspect of software security. Accurate detection is essential to prevent potential security breaches and protect software systems from malicious attacks. Recently, vulnerability detection methods leveraging deep learning and large language models (LLMs) have garnered increasing attention. However, existing approaches often focus on analyzing individual files or functions, which limits their ability to gather sufficient contextual information. Analyzing entire repositories to gather context introduces significant noise and computational overhead. To address these challenges, we propose a context-enhanced vulnerability detection approach that combines program analysis with LLMs. Specifically, we use program analysis to extract contextual information at various levels of abstraction, thereby filtering out irrelevant noise. The abstracted context along with source code are provided to LLM for vulnerability detection. We investigate how different levels of contextual granularity improve LLM-based vulnerability detection performance. Our goal is to strike a balance between providing sufficient detail to accurately capture vulnerabilities and minimizing unnecessary complexity that could hinder model performance. Based on an extensive study using GPT-4, DeepSeek, and CodeLLaMA with various prompting strategies, our key findings includes: (1) incorporating abstracted context significantly enhances vulnerability detection effectiveness; (2) different models benefit from distinct levels of abstraction depending on their code understanding capabilities; and (3) capturing program behavior through program analysis for general LLM-based code analysis tasks can be a direction that requires further attention.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Context-Enhanced Vulnerability Detection Based on Large Language Model":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of improving vulnerability detection in software using Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors propose a context-enhanced approach called PacVD, which combines program analysis with LLMs. PacVD extracts contextual information at various levels of abstraction by analyzing the source code and identifying critical APIs using control flow and data flow analysis.  This context is then provided to the LLM, effectively filtering out irrelevant noise that might hinder model performance. The authors perform a comprehensive empirical study using GPT-4, DeepSeek, and CodeLLaMA, evaluating the impact of different levels of contextual granularity and prompting strategies on vulnerability detection performance. The key findings indicate that incorporating abstracted context significantly improves LLM-based vulnerability detection, the optimal abstraction level varies depending on the LLM, and specialized prompt engineering is essential for maximizing performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination of program analysis, API abstraction, and LLMs for vulnerability detection, especially the use of "primitive API abstraction." While previous work has explored using LLMs and program analysis separately or combining raw code from callees, the authors provide a targeted approach to extracting and presenting contextual information, balancing information richness with computational efficiency. This nuanced context extraction strategy is a novel component. The methodology for determining the level of the abstraction (fuzzy branch, concrete branch, etc.) is also a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** The paper makes a significant contribution by addressing a known limitation of LLMs in vulnerability detection: the need for sufficient context without introducing excessive noise. The systematic empirical evaluation provides valuable insights into the effectiveness of different abstraction levels and prompting strategies for various LLMs. The results have direct implications for practical vulnerability detection systems and can guide practitioners in selecting appropriate techniques and LLMs for their needs. Furthermore, the demonstration of competitive performance relative to complex "all callees" methodologies with focused API call extraction and analysis is notable.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The empirical study is thorough, using multiple LLMs, abstraction levels, prompting strategies, and a sizable dataset of real-world vulnerabilities.
    *   **Clear Research Questions:** The research questions are well-defined and address important aspects of LLM-based vulnerability detection.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The paper provides practical recommendations for practitioners and researchers based on the experimental results.
    *   **Strong Rationale:** The choices for API abstraction, prompt engineering approaches and LLM selection have a clear rationale and build on previous work.
    *   **Reproducible Results:** The authors make their datasets and experimental details publicly available, which promotes reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses primarily on C/C++ vulnerabilities. While this is a critical area, extending the approach to other programming languages could broaden its applicability.
    *   **Idealized Prompts:** Prompt engineering is a rapidly evolving field. The studied prompt strategies, while common, could become outdated quickly. The generalizability of the "best" prompt is also questionable across different contexts.
    *   **Reliance on Static Analysis:** The approach relies on static program analysis to extract contextual information. This can limit its ability to detect vulnerabilities that depend on dynamic program behavior.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of software security by providing a more effective and practical approach to LLM-based vulnerability detection. The insights gained from the empirical study can guide the development of future vulnerability detection tools and techniques. The API abstraction method could inspire similar context extraction approaches in other areas of software analysis.

The PacVD approach provides a balanced strategy of context retrieval and analysis that may improve the consistency and practicality of LLM-based vulnerability analysis. The novel method of program analysis-driven context extraction is a significant element.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of vulnerability detection. While it has some limitations (scope and reliance on static analysis), the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. The comprehensive evaluation, clear research questions, actionable insights, and strong rationale make it a valuable addition to the literature. The findings have the potential to influence practical vulnerability detection systems and inspire further research in this area.  The ability to maintain high recall while improving precision is a notable factor for practical deployment.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### Do Large Language Models know who did what to whom?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16884v1)
- **Authors**: Joseph M. Denning, Xiaohan, Guo, Bryor Snefjella, Idan A. Blank
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are commonly criticized for not understanding language. However, many critiques focus on cognitive abilities that, in humans, are distinct from language processing. Here, we instead study a kind of understanding tightly linked to language: inferring who did what to whom (thematic roles) in a sentence. Does the central training objective of LLMs-word prediction-result in sentence representations that capture thematic roles? In two experiments, we characterized sentence representations in four LLMs. In contrast to human similarity judgments, in LLMs the overall representational similarity of sentence pairs reflected syntactic similarity but not whether their agent and patient assignments were identical vs. reversed. Furthermore, we found little evidence that thematic role information was available in any subset of hidden units. However, some attention heads robustly captured thematic roles, independently of syntax. Therefore, LLMs can extract thematic roles but, relative to humans, this information influences their representations more weakly.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper abstract and partial manuscript:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) can infer thematic roles ("who did what to whom") from sentence structure, even though they are trained primarily on word prediction. Through two experiments, the authors evaluate how LLMs represent sentence similarity based on syntax and thematic role assignments, comparing their performance to human judgments. The findings suggest that LLMs prioritize syntactic similarity over thematic role similarity in their internal representations. While thematic role information is not reliably present in the models' hidden units, some attention heads robustly capture thematic roles independently of syntax. Therefore, LLMs extract thematic roles, but this information has less influence on their representations compared to humans.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's focus on thematic role assignment as a measure of understanding in LLMs is valuable. While previous work has explored semantic capabilities of LLMs, focusing specifically on this core linguistic function and contrasting it explicitly with syntactic processing offers a relatively fresh perspective. The approach of probing internal representations and attention heads, rather than solely relying on prompting, is also a strength, especially with the inclusion of a strong emphasis on controlled experimental design. However, the paper does not present a radically new methodology. The analysis techniques (representational similarity analysis, SVM classification) are relatively standard within the field of computational linguistics and LLM research.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its direct comparison of LLM and human performance on a task central to human language understanding. The finding that LLMs prioritize syntax over thematic roles in their internal representations, in contrast to humans, has important implications for how we understand the differences in processing mechanisms and capabilities. Further, it offers a better understanding of what LLMs are capturing and what they are failing to model about human language processing. However, the extent to which LLMs are trained to achieve specific objectives is always changing, so the findings would be best considered as a benchmark to which other LLMs can be compared. The findings regarding the robustness of attention heads for capturing thematic roles offer insights into potentially more human-aligned components of the LLM architecture.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear research question and well-defined experimental design (manipulating thematic roles and syntax).
    *   Systematic comparison of LLM representations and human judgments.
    *   Investigation of both hidden units and attention heads, providing a more comprehensive picture.
    *   The emphasis on carefully crafted stimuli designed to be less susceptible to shortcuts or heuristics.
    *   Acknowledging and addressing potential limitations of the study.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on cosine similarity and linear SVMs may limit the ability to detect more complex or non-linear relationships within the data.
    *   The conclusion about the lesser influence of thematic roles in LLMs is primarily based on Experiment 1's overall representational similarity. While Experiment 2 investigates thematic role representations in subsets of hidden units and attention heads, the results, are not especially compelling in the hidden unit case.
    *   The paper's exploration is specific to models not fine-tuned on specific tasks, and it may not be generalizable to models like ChatGPT or GPT-4. This is a deliberate and justified choice, but it does limit the broad applicability of the findings.

*   **Potential Impact:**
    *   The paper can inform the development of more human-like language models by highlighting the need to prioritize thematic roles in their representations.
    *   It provides insights into the limitations of training LLMs solely on word prediction and the potential benefits of incorporating other learning objectives or architectural modifications.
    *   It stimulates further research into the role of attention mechanisms in capturing semantic information.
    *   It can provide a benchmark against which future more powerful LLMs can be compared for performance on thematic role identification.

**Score:** 6.5

**Rationale:** The paper makes a solid contribution to the understanding of LLMs by focusing on a core linguistic function and comparing the LLMs to human language processing. The investigation into different aspects of the LLM's architecture (hidden units and attention heads) improves the paper's comprehensiveness. However, the novelty of the methods is limited, and the results, while informative, could be interpreted as incremental progress rather than a groundbreaking discovery. The careful and focused experimental design lends itself well to future follow-up and expansion. Therefore, 6.5 is a fair reflection of the paper's merits, balancing originality and impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Tracing Thought: Using Chain-of-Thought Reasoning to Identify the LLM Behind AI-Generated Text
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16913v1)
- **Authors**: Shifali Agrahari, Sanasam Ranbir Singh
- **Abstract**: In recent years, the detection of AI-generated text has become a critical area of research due to concerns about academic integrity, misinformation, and ethical AI deployment. This paper presents COT Fine-tuned, a novel framework for detecting AI-generated text and identifying the specific language model. responsible for generating the text. We propose a dual-task approach, where Task A involves classifying text as AI-generated or human-written, and Task B identifies the specific LLM behind the text. The key innovation of our method lies in the use of Chain-of-Thought reasoning, which enables the model to generate explanations for its predictions, enhancing transparency and interpretability. Our experiments demonstrate that COT Fine-tuned achieves high accuracy in both tasks, with strong performance in LLM identification and human-AI classification. We also show that the CoT reasoning process contributes significantly to the models effectiveness and interpretability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces COT_Finetuned, a novel framework for detecting AI-generated text and identifying the specific Large Language Model (LLM) that created it. The framework employs a dual-task approach: Task A classifies text as AI-generated or human-written, while Task B identifies the LLM responsible for AI-generated content. The core innovation lies in leveraging Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning, enabling the model to generate explanations for its predictions, thus enhancing transparency and interpretability. Experimental results demonstrate high accuracy in both tasks, with strong performance in LLM identification and human-AI classification. The authors also highlight the significant contribution of the CoT reasoning process to the model's effectiveness. The paper compares their approach with BERT and RoBERTa, both with and without CoT.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper introduces a conceptually sound framework, COT_Finetuned, which attempts to simultaneously detect AI-generated text and identify the source LLM. The use of Chain-of-Thought reasoning to provide explanations is a valuable addition, contributing towards greater interpretability.

*   **Significance:** The problem addressed by the paper is undoubtedly significant, considering the growing concerns around AI-generated content and its potential misuse.  A system capable of not only detecting but also tracing the origin of AI-generated text would have important applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The use of CoT reasoning for explainability is a strong aspect, offering a potential way to understand the model's decision-making process.
    *   The dual-task approach is sensible, addressing both detection and source identification.
    *   The reported results on a real-world dataset is a definite plus.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experimental section requires much more details. The description of the hyperparameter choices (50-250 epochs and a batch size of 32) seems arbitrary without a clear hyperparameter sweep methodology discussed.
    *   The datasets used in the study (Train/Test/Validation set) are not detailed in the paper. Additional details on the composition and type of texts would improve the quality.
    *   The presentation of the results is unclear. Tables 2 and 3 should be merged to make better comparisons.
    *   The paper would be strengthened by a detailed analysis of the failure modes of the model (what kinds of AI-generated text are still difficult to detect? Is the model biased towards identifying certain models over others?) and a study of different prompts to trigger different behaviors. This helps to understand the model's robustness.
    *   Comparison of results must be more rigorous. Showing how the COT reasoning process helps by providing example outputs would increase understanding.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by highlighting the importance of explainability in AI detection systems and promoting the use of CoT reasoning.

**Score:** 5.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable idea with a strong emphasis on interpretability (CoT reasoning). The problem being addressed is very relevant to current concerns about the ethical use of LLMs. However, the paper is held back by a somewhat limited novelty. While the CoT method is applied, the experiments are not strong enough and the evaluation lacks the depth needed to fully assess its effectiveness. A rigorous ablation study demonstrating the impact of each component (e.g., the use of dual tasks) would significantly strengthen the contributions. Moreover, the paper does not thoroughly analyze the limitations of the approach, and details around the experimental setup are rather vague. These shortcomings lead to a moderate score, awaiting more substantial evidence of the method's robustness and practical impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### IberBench: LLM Evaluation on Iberian Languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16921v1)
- **Authors**: José Ángel González, Ian Borrego Obrador, Álvaro Romo Herrero, Areg Mikael Sarvazyan, Mara Chinea-Ríos, Angelo Basile, Marc Franco-Salvador
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) remain difficult to evaluate comprehensively, particularly for languages other than English, where high-quality data is often limited. Existing benchmarks and leaderboards are predominantly English-centric, with only a few addressing other languages. These benchmarks fall short in several key areas: they overlook the diversity of language varieties, prioritize fundamental Natural Language Processing (NLP) capabilities over tasks of industrial relevance, and are static. With these aspects in mind, we present IberBench, a comprehensive and extensible benchmark designed to assess LLM performance on both fundamental and industry-relevant NLP tasks, in languages spoken across the Iberian Peninsula and Ibero-America. IberBench integrates 101 datasets from evaluation campaigns and recent benchmarks, covering 22 task categories such as sentiment and emotion analysis, toxicity detection, and summarization. The benchmark addresses key limitations in current evaluation practices, such as the lack of linguistic diversity and static evaluation setups by enabling continual updates and community-driven model and dataset submissions moderated by a committee of experts. We evaluate 23 LLMs ranging from 100 million to 14 billion parameters and provide empirical insights into their strengths and limitations. Our findings indicate that (i) LLMs perform worse on industry-relevant tasks than in fundamental ones, (ii) performance is on average lower for Galician and Basque, (iii) some tasks show results close to random, and (iv) in other tasks LLMs perform above random but below shared task systems. IberBench offers open-source implementations for the entire evaluation pipeline, including dataset normalization and hosting, incremental evaluation of LLMs, and a publicly accessible leaderboard.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the IberBench paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces IberBench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate Large Language Models (LLMs) on Iberian languages (Spanish, Portuguese, Catalan, Basque, Galician, and various Spanish varieties). It addresses the limitations of existing benchmarks, which are often English-centric, lack linguistic diversity, prioritize fundamental NLP tasks over industry-relevant ones, and are static. IberBench comprises 101 datasets from evaluation campaigns and recent benchmarks, covering 22 task categories. The benchmark features open-source implementations, dataset normalization, incremental evaluation, and a public leaderboard. The authors evaluate 23 LLMs ranging from 100 million to 14 billion parameters and analyze their performance across various tasks and languages.  Key findings include that LLMs perform worse on industry-relevant tasks, struggle with Galician and Basque, and in some tasks, perform barely above random.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in: (1) the *specific focus* on Iberian languages and their varieties, which are often underrepresented in existing LLM benchmarks; (2) the *inclusion of industry-relevant tasks* alongside fundamental NLP tasks, (3) its *dynamic design*, which enables continual updates and community contributions, thereby addressing the static nature of most current benchmarks, and (4) the collection and standardization of numerous datasets from previously scattered workshops.
*   **Significance:** The significance of IberBench stems from its potential to:
    *   *Improve LLM evaluation* by providing a more comprehensive and representative assessment of LLMs' capabilities in Iberian languages.
    *   *Drive LLM development* by highlighting the specific challenges posed by these languages and tasks, encouraging researchers to focus on areas where improvement is needed.
    *   *Promote the use of LLMs* by offering a tool that helps users choose the right LLM for their specific needs in Iberian language contexts.
    *   *Foster collaboration* within the Iberian NLP community by providing a shared resource and platform for evaluation and development.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   *Comprehensive dataset:* The collection and standardization of 101 datasets from diverse sources is a significant contribution.
    *   *Open-source implementation:* Making the evaluation pipeline open-source promotes reproducibility and facilitates community involvement.
    *   *Dynamic design:* The ability to update and extend the benchmark ensures its long-term relevance.
    *   *Focus on both fundamental and practical tasks:* It differentiates IberBench from other benchmarks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   *Zero-shot evaluation (mostly):* While justifiable, it might underestimate the potential of some models in few-shot settings.
    *   *Limited language representation:* Certain Spanish varieties and Brazilian Portuguese are still underrepresented.
    *   *Dependence on ROUGE-1:* For text summarization, a more sophisticated metric would be preferable.
    *   *The number of models evaluated, whilst significant, could benefit from a greater breadth of closed and open-source, smaller and larger sized LLMs.*

*   **Potential Influence:** IberBench has a high potential to influence the field by:
    *   *Setting a new standard* for LLM evaluation in Iberian languages.
    *   *Guiding future research* by highlighting the challenges and opportunities in this area.
    *   *Serving as a valuable resource* for both researchers and practitioners working with Iberian languages.
    *   *Promoting the development and adoption of LLMs* in these languages.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**
IberBench is a valuable contribution to the field of NLP, particularly for Iberian languages. It addresses critical gaps in existing benchmarks, provides a comprehensive and extensible evaluation framework, and has the potential to drive significant progress in LLM development. Its strengths outweigh its weaknesses, making it a valuable resource for the Iberian NLP community.

While not a groundbreaking theoretical advance, the *practical value* and *community impact* of IberBench are substantial. IberBench fills a clear need and is likely to become a key resource for LLM evaluation and development in the Iberian language space.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unsupervised Time-Series Signal Analysis with Autoencoders and Vision Transformers: A Review of Architectures and Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16972v1)
- **Authors**: Hossein Ahmadi, Sajjad Emdadi Mahdimahalleh, Arman Farahat, Banafsheh Saffari
- **Abstract**: The rapid growth of unlabeled time-series data in domains such as wireless communications, radar, biomedical engineering, and the Internet of Things (IoT) has driven advancements in unsupervised learning. This review synthesizes recent progress in applying autoencoders and vision transformers for unsupervised signal analysis, focusing on their architectures, applications, and emerging trends. We explore how these models enable feature extraction, anomaly detection, and classification across diverse signal types, including electrocardiograms, radar waveforms, and IoT sensor data. The review highlights the strengths of hybrid architectures and self-supervised learning, while identifying challenges in interpretability, scalability, and domain generalization. By bridging methodological innovations and practical applications, this work offers a roadmap for developing robust, adaptive models for signal intelligence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper is a review that synthesizes recent advancements in applying unsupervised learning techniques, specifically Autoencoders (AEs) and Vision Transformers (ViTs), to time-series signal analysis across various domains like wireless communications, radar systems, IoT, and biomedical engineering.  It explores the architectures, applications, and emerging trends of these models, highlighting their use in feature extraction, anomaly detection, and classification.
